which concludes the proof of Claim
3.3
and, hence, Lemma
2.1
is established.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Theorem
3.1
from
[
3
]
is more general and applies not only to
cliques
K_{\ell}
, but to all
strictly balanced
graphs
H
for

p\geq Cn^{-\frac{|V(H)|-2}{|E(H)|-1}}
, i.e., for graphs
H
satisfying

\frac{|E(H^{\prime})|-1}{|V(H^{\prime})|-2}<\frac{|E(H)|-1}{|V(H)|-2}

for all proper subgraphs
H^{\prime}\subsetneq H
on at least three vertices.
Starting with this general version of Theorem
3.1
, the arguments
tfrom this section can be carried out
verbatim for such graphs
H
. This yields a version of Lemma
2.1
guaranteeing
rainbow copies of strictly balanced graphs
H
for
(\delta,p)
-bounded edge colourings
of
G(n,p)
for
p\geq Cn^{-\frac{|V(H)|-2}{|E(H)|-1}}
as long as
\delta
is sufficiently
small and
C
is sufficiently large depending on
H
.

4.
Canonical cliques in unbounded colourings of random graphs

This section contains the proof of Lemma
2.2
along with the required prerequisites.
We begin with an overview of the proof.

4.1.
Outline of the proof

Again, the main tool in the proof of Lemma
2.2
is the transference principle developed by Conlon and Gowers.
This result asserts that asymptotically almost surely every subgraph
F

of
G(n,p)
has a dense model (see, e.g., Theorem
3.1

( )
‣
3.1
).
Moreover, if
p\gg n^{-\frac{2}{\ell+1}}
, then the dense model
D
and the subgraph
F
,
have closely related distributions
of
the copies of
K_{\ell}
, which is made precise
in part
( )
‣
3.1
of Theorem
3.1
. Roughly speaking,
we may think of
F
being
close to
a random subgraph of
D
whose edges are sampled independently with probability
p
.

For mimicking the argument from Section
2
for colourings of
K_{n}
, the main obstacle is that the neighbourhoods
in
G(n,p)
are of order
pn
, not allowing the iteration rendered in (
2.2
).
We circumvent this by considering
suitable
subgraphs
F_{1},F_{2},\ldots,F_{L}
of
G(n,p)
for
L=2(\ell-1)(\ell-2)+2

and obtain their dense models
D_{1},\ldots,D_{L}
, which yield linear-sized sets as neighbourhoods in the dense models.
Another challenge is that, for transference to be useful, the dense model has to contain
\Omega(n^{\ell})
copies of
K_{\ell}
,
so we also need a robust, counting version of the argument for the case of unbounded colourings of
K_{n}
.

In the proof of Lemma
2.2
the ordering of the underlying vertex set will be important. For that, we refine
the definition of
d_{c}(u,U)
for a given edge colouring
\varphi
and for
\diamond\in\{<\,,>\}
we set

d^{\diamond}_{c}(u,U)=\big|N^{\diamond}_{c}(u,U)\big|=\big|\{w\in U\colon\varphi(uw)=c\ \text{and}\ u\diamond w\}\big|\,.

In Lemma
2.2
we assume that every sufficiently large set
U
of vertices of the random graph has the property that
half of its vertices
u
have a large monochromatic neighbourhood. For simplicity, in the outline below we shall always
assume that, in fact, these vertices
u
always lie before their monochromatic neighbourhood,
i.e.,
d^{<}_{c}(u,U)\geq 4\delta p|U|
for at least
|U|/4
vertices.

The subgraphs
F_{i}\subseteq G(n,p)
for
i\in[L]
will be selected in an iterative manner. For the definition of
F_{1}
,
let
V_{1}
be those
n/4
vertices
v
such that
d^{<}_{c(v)}(v)\geq 4\delta pn
for some associated colour
c(v)
.
The graph
F_{1}
is then defined as the union of those edges, i.e.,

E(F_{1})=\{vw\in E(G(n,p))\colon v\in V_{1},v<w,\ \text{and}\ \varphi(vw)=c(v)\}\,.

In other words, the colour of each edge
e\in E(F_{1})
is defined by its
starting point

\min(e)
.
The transference principle yields a dense model
D_{1}
of
F_{1}
, and by part
( )
‣
3.1
of Theorem
3.1

most
v\in V_{1}
satisfy
p\cdot d^{<}_{D_{1}}(v)
is well approximated by
d^{<}_{F_{1}}(v)
. Consequently, typically
the neighbourhood of
v
in
D_{1}
defines a subset
S_{1}(v)
of size at least
3\delta n
.

We continue and define
F_{2}
based on the sets
S_{1}(v_{1})
for
v_{1}\in V_{1}
. Again appealing to the
assumption (
2.3
) of Lemma
2.2
as described above for
U=S_{1}(v_{1})
,
we obtain
|S_{1}(v_{1})|/4
pairs
(v_{2},c)\in S_{1}(v_{1})\times\mathds{N}
with

(4.1)

d_{c}^{<}(v_{2},S_{1}(v_{1}))\geq 4\delta p|S_{1}(v_{1})|=\Omega(\delta^{2}pn)\,.

However, the colour
c
depends on
v_{1}
and, similarly, as in the definition of
F_{1}
we shall find a “large”
monochromatic neighbourhood of
v_{2}
for the definition of
F_{2}
. On the other hand, since the degree of

v_{2}
is close to
pn
in
G(n,p)
at most
1/\delta^{2}
different colours might occur for different
choices of
v_{1}
. We let
c(v_{2})
be the
majority
colour and restrict to it for the definition of
F_{2}
.
Moreover, for an appropriately chosen subset
V_{2}\subseteq\bigcup\{S_{1}(v_{1})\colon v_{1}\in V_{1}\}
and
we shall define
F_{2}
in such a way that

E(F_{2})\subseteq\{v_{2}w\colon v_{2}\in V_{2},v_{2}<w,\ \text{and}\ \varphi(v_{2}w)=c(v_{2})\}\,.

In particular, the colours of the edges in
F_{2}
are determined again by their starting point. For some technical reasons the definition of
F_{2}
is a bit more involved and, for example, we will impose
F_{2}
to be bipartite (see Claim
4.5
below for the formal statement).
Similar as before, we obtain a dense model
D_{2}
for
F_{2}
. Owing to (
4.1
),
the vertices in
V_{2}
have
\Omega(\delta^{2}pn)
neighbours in
F_{2}
. Consequently, most vertices in
V_{2}
have
\Omega(\delta^{2}n)
neighbours
in
D_{2}
. Those neighbourhoods yield the linear sized sets
S_{2}(v_{2})
, which allows us to iterate the argument to obtain a subgraph
F_{3}\subseteq G(n,p)
with all edges colours determined by its starting vertex and a dense model
D_{3}
of
F_{3}
with linear sized neighbourhoods.

This way we obtain graphs
F=F_{1}\cup\dots\cup F_{L}
, and
D=D_{1}\cup\dots\cup D_{L}
. We shall show that the dense graph

D=
contains
\Omega(n^{\ell})
copies of
K_{\ell}
by its construction through “nested neighbourhoods.” Consequently,
the transference principle in form of part
( )
‣
3.1
of Theorem
3.1

tells us that the sparse subgraph
F\subseteq G(n,p)

contains
\Omega(p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell})
copies of
K_{\ell}
and by the choice of the
F_{i}
these copies will be
non-strictly min-coloured.

It remains to analyse the induced vertex colourings of these non-strictly min-coloured copies of
K_{\ell}
in
G(n,p)
.
However, those induced vertex colours are not synchronised within each of the sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{L}
, i.e., the edges edges of
F_{i}

are not monochromatic and may induce different colours for the vertices in
V_{i}
.
We address this issue by a more careful selection of the graphs
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}

(see
( )
‣
4.5
of Claim
4.5
below).

The proof of Lemma
2.2
is based on a more involved application of the transference principle compared to the proof of Lemma
2.1
in Section
3
. In Section
4.2
we review the required results from
[
4
]

and some helpful consequences for the proof of Lemma
2.2
presented in Section
4.3
.

4.2.
The transference principle

In this section, we present the prerequisites for the proof of Lemma
2.2
. The statements are written for arbitrary strictly balanced graphs
H
, although we shall only employ them for
H=K_{\ell}
. For a strictly balanced graph
H
on at least three vertices,
we can define its
2
-density
by

m_{2}(H)=\frac{|E(H)|-1}{|V(H)|-2}\,,

and we note that
m_{2}(K_{\ell})=(\ell+1)/2
appears in the exponents of
p=p(n)
in the in the assumptions of the earlier
statements in Sections
1
–
3
.
Similarly, below we shall impose
p=p(n)\geq Cn^{-1/m_{2}(H)}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
for some
strictly balanced graph
H
on the vertex set
[\ell]
for some
\ell\geq 3
.

For some (large) integer
n
, we shall work within the set of functions from
[n]^{(2)}

to
\mathds{R}
, which naturally corresponds to the set of
weighted graphs
on the vertex set
[n]
.
Therefore, we often identify a graph
F
on
[n]
with the indicator function
\mathds{1}_{F}
of its edge set and a
dense model
\mathfrak{d}
for
F
is a function
\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
,
which is “close” to
p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}

in terms of its distribution
of
weighted edges and copies of
H
.
The definitions of edge counts and vertex degrees extend straightforwardly to functions

\mathfrak{f}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
and for that we set

e(\mathfrak{f})=\sum_{u<w}\mathfrak{f}(uw)\,,\quad e_{\mathfrak{f}}(U,W)=\sum_{u\in U,w\in W}\mathfrak{f}(uw)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad d_{\mathfrak{f}}(v,U)=\sum_{u\in U\smallsetminus\{v\}}\mathfrak{f}(uv).

With this notation at hand we can define the
cut-norm
by

\left\|\mathfrak{f}\right\|_{\square}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\max_{U,W\subseteq[n]}e_{\mathfrak{f}}(U,W)\,.

This norm allows us to compare the edge distributions of two weighted graphs on
[n]
. In fact, we will consider
weighted graphs
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{d}
to be “close,” if
\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}
is “small” (see, e.g.,
Theorem
3.1

( )
‣
3.1
and Theorem
4.1

( )
‣
4.1
below).
Similarly, for a graph
H
with vertex set
[\ell]
we define its homomorphism density in
\mathfrak{f}
by

\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{f})=\frac{1}{n^{\ell}}\sum_{v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}\in[n]}\prod_{ij\in e(H)}\mathfrak{f}(v_{i}v_{j})\,,

where we use the convention
\mathfrak{f}(v_{i}v_{j})=0
in case
v_{i}=v_{j}
. Consequently, for cliques the quantity

\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(\mathfrak{f})
corresponds to the weighted
K_{\ell}
-density in
\mathfrak{f}
and for
\mathfrak{f}
being an unweighted
graph we can recover the notation
\kappa_{\ell}(\mathfrak{f})=n^{\ell}\cdot\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(\mathfrak{f})
from Section
3
.

As discussed in the outline for the proof of Lemma
2.2
,
Theorem
3.1
will be applied in stages to the
subgraphs
F_{1},\dots,F_{L}\subseteq G(n,p)
to obtain dense models
D_{1},\dots,D_{L}
.
In order to ensure that
D=D_{1}\cup\dots\cup D_{L}
is still a useful approximation
of
F=F_{1}\cup\dots\cup F_{L}
, we need a bit more insight into how these dense models are obtained.
Informally, Conlon and Gowers
[
4
]
construct a norm
\|\cdot\|
on the set of weighted graphs

\mathds{R}^{[n]^{(2)}}
so that the following holds:
If
\mathds{1}_{F}
is the characteristic function of the edges of some

F\subseteq G(n,p)
and
\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}-\mathfrak{d}\|
is sufficiently small for some dense model
\mathfrak{d}
with

\|\mathfrak{d}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, then
p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}
and
\mathfrak{d}
have a “similar”
distribution of edges and copies of
H
.
A major contribution of
[
4
]
is precisely finding a norm which is sufficiently weak to allow a dense model which is arbitrarily close to
p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}
, and sufficiently strong to preserve the relevant properties of
F
. However, the norm
\|\cdot\|
actually depends on the random graph
G(n,p)

in the sense that asymptotically almost surely
G\in G(n,p)
has the property that there is a norm
\|\cdot\|
with the aforementioned
properties for every subgraph
F\subseteq G
. Theorem
4.1
below is version of the transference principle of Conlon and Gowers, which is tailored for our proof of Lemma
2.2
. It is implicit
in the work in
[
4
]
and in the Appendix we discuss in more detail how it can be extracted.

Theorem 4.1
.

For every strictly balanced graph
H
with
V(H)=[\ell]
and every
\varepsilon>0
there is some constant
C>0
such that for
p=p(n)
with

Cn^{-1/m_{2}(H)}\leq p\leq 1/C
asymptotically almost surely the following holds for
G\in G(n,p)
.

There exists a norm
\|\cdot\|
on the set of weighted graphs
\mathds{R}^{[n]^{(2)}}
such that
for every
F\subseteq G
, there is a dense model
\mathfrak{d}_{F}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with
\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}_{F}\|<\varepsilon

and

(

)

for all functions
\mathfrak{f}
,
\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
with
\|\mathfrak{f}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}\|<\varepsilon
we have

\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}\leq 2\varepsilon\,.

(

)

for every function
\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,2\ell]
with
\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}\|\leq\varepsilon
we have

\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{d})\leq p^{-|E(H)|}\Lambda_{H}(F)+(4\ell)^{\ell^{2}}\cdot\varepsilon\,.

We emphasise that, while
( )
‣
4.1
applies to any weighted graph
\mathfrak{f}
,
part
( )
‣
4.1
only applies to the subgraphs
F
of the random graph.
Anyhow, in our intended application we have
\mathfrak{f}=p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}
. Theorem
4.1
is closely related
to Theorem
3.1
where
S
and
D
in Theorem
3.1
take the rôle of
F
and
\mathfrak{d}_{F}

in Theorem
4.1
. In fact, applying
( )
‣
4.1
and
( )
‣
4.1

with
\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{d}_{F}
and
\mathfrak{f}=p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}
in Theorem
4.1
yields closely related statements
( )
‣
3.1

and
( )
‣
3.1
of Theorem
3.1
.

It will be convenient to move from the dense weighted graphs
\mathfrak{d}
back to unweighted graphs
D
sampled by the edge weights of
\mathfrak{d}
.
The following lemma follows directly from the sharp concentration of the binomial distribution and Chernoff’s inequality.

Lemma 4.2
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
and any sequence of functions
\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
asymptotically almost surely we have

\left\|\mathds{1}_{D}-\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}\leq\varepsilon
for the random graph
D
on
[n]
with every edge
e
appearing independently
with probability
\mathfrak{d}(e)
. ∎

We will also use the following
counting lemma
comparing the number of copies of subgraph of graphs in
terms of the cut-norm. This can be viewed as the global counting lemma for the weak regularity lemma
of Frieze and Kannan
[
9
]
and it can be found in
[
14
]
*Lemma 4.1.

Lemma 4.3
.

For every graph
H
and all functions
\mathfrak{f}
,
\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
we have

\big|\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{f})-\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{d})\big|\leq 2e(H)\cdot\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}\qed

We conclude this section with the following fact that the cut-norm controls most vertex degrees into given subsets
(see, e.g., the deduction of
( )
‣
3.1
of Theorem
3.1
from
( )
‣
3.1
).

Lemma 4.4
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
and all functions
\mathfrak{f}
,
\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with
\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}\leq\varepsilon

the following holds.
For all
U\subseteq[n]
with
|U|>2\varepsilon^{1/3}n
, all but at most
\varepsilon^{1/3}n
vertices
v\in[n]

satisfy

|d_{\mathfrak{f}}(v,U)-d_{\mathfrak{d}}(v,U)|\leq\varepsilon^{1/3}|U|\,.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Let
S
be the set of vertices
v
with
d_{\mathfrak{f}}(v,U)-d_{\mathfrak{d}}(v,U)\geq\varepsilon^{1/3}|U|
.
We have

\displaystyle n^{2}\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}\geq\sum_{v\in S}d_{\mathfrak{f}}(v,U)-d_{\mathfrak{d}}(v,U)\geq\varepsilon^{1/3}|U||S|\geq 2\varepsilon^{2/3}n|S|

and
|S|\leq\varepsilon^{1/3}n/2
folllows. Similarly, one can show that there are at most
\varepsilon^{1/3}n/2
vertices
v
such that
d_{\mathfrak{d}}(v,U)-d_{\mathfrak{f}}(v,U)\geq\varepsilon^{1/3}|U|
and the claimed bound follows.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Lemma
2.2

Given
\ell\geq 3
and
\delta>0
we fix auxiliary constants

L=2(\ell-1)(\ell-2)+2\,,\quad\nu=\frac{\delta^{4\ell^{4}+2\ell^{2}}}{10^{4\ell^{2}}}\,,\quad\alpha=\frac{\nu}{4\ell^{2}}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta=\delta^{10\ell^{2}+1}\,.

Moreover we fix auxiliary constants
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
and the desired
C
to satisfy the hierarchy

\delta\,,\ell^{-1}\gg\varepsilon_{2}\gg\varepsilon_{1}\gg C^{-1}\,.

Assume that
G\in G(n,p)
satisfies the conclusion of Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
3.1

( )
‣
3.1

for
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{1}
. Let
\left\|\,\cdot\,\right\|
denote the norm given by Theorem
4.1
.
Moreover, we may assume that all vertices in
G
have degree
(1\pm\varepsilon_{1})pn
, since all these properties hold asymptotically almost surely.

For the moreover-part of the lemma,
we have in addition
p=p(n)\leq n^{-\frac{2\ell-2}{\ell^{2}+\ell-4}}/\omega(n)
for some function

\omega
tending to infinity. Similarly as in the proof of Lemma
2.1
this implies that we can assume

(4.2)

\big|E(G)\smallsetminus E(G_{\ell})\big|\leq\zeta pn^{2}\,.

By our choice of
\zeta
this implies that the crucial assumption (
2.3
) for
G
in
Lemma
2.2
extends to
G_{\ell}
on the price of a small change of the constants and the proof given below
can be carried out in
G_{\ell}
as well.

For a comparability sign
\diamond\in\{<,\,>\}
set

B^{\diamond}(U)=\{v\in U\colon d_{c}^{\diamond}(v,U)\geq 4\delta p|U|\text{ for some colour }c\}\,.

The assumption (
2.3
) implies that for every set
U
with
|U|\geq\delta^{5\ell^{2}}n
, we have

(4.3)

|B^{<}(U)|\geq\frac{|U|}{4}\quad\text{ \ or \ }\quad|B^{>}(U)|\geq\frac{|U|}{4}.

The condition (
4.3
) will be iterated to inductively build some structures in
G
, as detailed in the following claim. We will build subgraphs
F_{t}
of
G
which are non-strictly min-coloured or max-coloured and
relatively dense
to
G
, and
D_{t}
will be dense models of
F_{t}
obtained using Theorem
4.1
. The hypergraph
{\mathcal{H}}
is used to keep track of cliques in
\bigcup_{t}D_{t}
. In the statement and proof, we usually identify a hypergraph
{\mathcal{H}}
with its set of edges. In particular,
|{\mathcal{H}}|
is the number of edges in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Claim 4.5
.

For every
t\in[L]
there is a set of vertices
V_{t}
with
|V_{t}|\geq\frac{1}{25}\alpha\delta^{2t}
,
a comparability sign
\diamond_{t}\in\{<,\,>\}
, and a colour index
\psi_{t}\in\mathds{N}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\star\}

such that the following holds:

(

)

Each
v\in V_{t}
is assigned a colour
c(v)
and there is a graph
F_{t}\subseteq G

whose edges
vu
satisfy
v\in V_{t}
and
u\in N^{\diamond_{t}}_{c(v)}(v)\smallsetminus(V_{1}\cup\dots\cup V_{t})
.

(

)

If
\psi_{t}=j\in\mathds{N}
, then
c(v)=j
for all
v\in V_{t}
. Otherwise, if
\psi_{t}=\star
, then we have
|\{v\in V_{t}\colon c(v)=j\}|\leq\alpha|V_{t}|
for every colour
j
.

(

)

There is a function
\mathfrak{d}_{t}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with

\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{t}}-(1+\varepsilon_{1})\mathfrak{d}_{t}\|\leq\varepsilon_{1}\,,

and a graph
D_{t}
with

\left\|D_{t}-\mathfrak{d}_{t}\right\|_{\square}\leq 7\varepsilon_{1}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand \hskip 10.22217pt}\left\|D_{t}-p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{t}}\right\|_{\square}\leq 9\varepsilon_{1}\,.

(

)

There is a
t
-partite
t
-uniform hypergraph
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
on
V_{1}\cup\dots\cup V_{t}
with

|{\mathcal{H}}_{t}|\geq 10^{-2t}\delta^{t^{2}}|V_{1}|\dots|V_{t}|

and for any
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
, there is a set
S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t})
disjoint from
V_{1},\ldots,V_{t}
of size at least
\delta^{t}n
such that for
i\leq t
,

N_{D_{i}}(v_{i})\supseteq\{v_{i+1},\ldots,v_{t}\}\cup S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t})\,.

Proof of Claim
4.5
.

To show the induction basis, let
t=1
.
Let
B=B^{\diamond_{1}}([n])
be the set of
\frac{n}{4}
vertices given
by (
4.3
). For
v\in B
, let
c(v)
be a colour in which

d^{\diamond_{1}}_{c(v)}(v)\geq 4\delta pn,

Moreover, we wish to transfer a
bipartite
graph
F_{1}
. To this end, let each vertex of
B
be placed into a set
Z_{1}
independently at random with probability
1/2
, and let
Y_{1}=[n]\smallsetminus Z_{1}
. We may assume that
|Z_{1}|\geq|B|/3\geq n/30
, and each
v\in Z_{1}
satisfies

(4.4)

d^{\diamond_{1}}_{c(v)}(v,Y_{1})\geq 1.5\delta pn

as this happens with high probability.

If there is a colour
j
such that
|c^{-1}(j)\cap Z_{1}|\geq\alpha|Z_{1}|
, set
V_{1}=c^{-1}(j)
and
\psi_{1}=j
. Otherwise take
V_{1}=Z_{1}
and set
\psi_{1}=\star
. Note that in either case,

|V_{1}|\geq\frac{\alpha n}{30}\,.

Let
F_{1}
be the subgraph of
G
with

E(F_{1})=\{v_{1}v\colon v_{1}\in V_{1},v\in Y_{1},v_{1}\diamond_{1}v,\ \text{and}\ \varphi(v_{1}v)=c(v_{1})\}\,.

Let
\mathds{1}_{F_{1}}
be the characteristic function of
F_{1}
. By Theorem
4.1
, there is a weighted graph

\mathfrak{d}_{1}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with
\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{1}}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}\|\leq\varepsilon_{1}
. In particular,

\left\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{1}}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}\right\|_{\square}\leq 2\varepsilon_{1}
by Theorem
4.1

( )
‣
4.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{d}_{1}\!\restriction_{V_{1}\times([n]\smallsetminus V_{1})}
. Passing to a restriction of
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
is just a technicality to circumvent small overlaps between
\mathfrak{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{d}_{t}
, and we will now show that
\left\|\mathfrak{d}_{1}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}\right\|_{\square}\leq 6\varepsilon_{1}
. Denote
\mathfrak{f}_{1}=p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{1}}
. To bound
\mathfrak{d}_{1}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}
, notice that, by definition of
\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}
and since all edges of
F_{1}
lie in
V_{1}\times([n]\smallsetminus V_{1})
,

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}}([n])-e_{\mathfrak{f}_{1}}([n])\right)=\frac{2}{n^{2}}\left(e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}(V_{1},[n]\smallsetminus V_{1})-e_{\mathfrak{f}_{1}}(V_{1},[n]\smallsetminus V_{1})\right)\leq 2\left\|\mathfrak{d}_{1}-\mathfrak{f}_{1}\right\|_{\square}\leq 4\varepsilon_{1}\,.

Hence
e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}([n])-e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}}([n])=e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}([n])-e_{\mathfrak{f}_{1}}([n])+e_{\mathfrak{f}_{1}}([n])-e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}}([n])\leq 6\varepsilon_{1}n^{2}
. Since
\mathfrak{d}_{1}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}\geq 0
, we have

\left\|\mathfrak{d}_{1}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}\right\|_{\square}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}(e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}([n])-e_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}}([n]))\leq 6\varepsilon_{1}.

Moreover,

\left\|\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}-p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{1}}\right\|_{\square}\leq 8\varepsilon_{1}

using the triangle inequality.

Let
D_{1}
be a graph sampled from
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{1}
. With high probability,
\left\|\mathds{1}_{D_{1}}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\prime}\right\|_{\square}\leq\varepsilon_{1}
by Lemma
4.2
. Therefore, using the triangle inequality, we may assume that

\left\|D_{1}-\mathfrak{d}_{1}\right\|_{\square}\leq 7\varepsilon_{1}\text{\quad and \quad}\left\|D_{1}-p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{1}}\right\|_{\square}\leq 9\varepsilon_{1}\,.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
be the set of vertices
v\in V_{1}
with

d_{D_{1}}(v,Y_{1})\geq\delta n\,.

By Lemma
4.4
, using (
4.4
),
\left\|\mathds{1}_{D_{1}}-p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{1}}\right\|_{\square}\leq 9\varepsilon_{1}
, and taking
\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon_{2}^{4}
, we have that
|{\mathcal{H}}_{1}|\geq|V_{1}|-\varepsilon_{2}n\geq\frac{|V_{1}|}{2}
. This completes the case
t=1
.

Suppose the Claim holds for
1,2,\ldots,t-1
. Let
X=[n]\smallsetminus(V_{1}\cup\dots\cup V_{t-1})
. For
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}
, consider the set
S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})\subseteq X
as stated in
( )
‣
4.5
. Specifically,
|S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})|\geq\delta^{t-1}n
. Denote
\xi=\xi(t):=\delta^{t-1}
. Applying the assumption (
4.3
), we obtain a set
B^{\diamond}(S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}))
of order at least
\frac{\xi n}{10}
for some
\diamond=\diamond(v_{1},\ldots v_{t-1})
such that for every
v\in B^{\diamond}(S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}))
and some colour
c=c(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1},v)
,

(4.5)

d^{\diamond}_{c}(v,S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}))\geq 4\delta\xi pn\,.

The next step is to remove the dependency of
\diamond
and
c
on
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})
. Firstly, let
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{t-1}
be a subhypergraph of
{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}
of order at least
\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|
such that
\diamond(v_{1},\ldots v_{t-1})=\diamond_{t}
for all
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{t-1}
.

Now form an auxiliary bipartite graph
J
with parts
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{t-1}
and
X\times\mathds{N}
as follows: an edge
((v_{1},v_{2},\ldots v_{t-1}),(v,c))
in
J
means that

(4.6)

\penalty\ d_{c}^{\diamond_{t}}(v,S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}))\geq 4\delta\xi pn\,.

Since every
(v_{1},\ldots v_{t-1})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{t-1}
is contained in at least
\xi n/10
edges in
J
(one for each element of
B^{\diamond_{t}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})
), we have

|J|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\cdot\frac{\xi n}{10}\,.

Let
X^{\prime}\subset X
be the set of vertices
v
such that some
(v,c)
is incident to an edge of
J
, and note that for each
v
, there are at most
(3\delta\xi)^{-1}
such colours
c
– this follows from
d_{c}(v)\geq 4\delta\xi pn
and
d_{G}(v)\leq(1+\varepsilon_{1})pn
.
For each
v\in X^{\prime}
, let
c(v)
be the colour which maximises the degree of
(v,c)
in
J
. Form
J^{\prime}
from
J
by deleting all the vertices
(v,c^{\dagger})
with
c^{\dagger}\neq c(v)
; we have

|J^{\prime}|\geq|J|\cdot 3\delta\xi\geq\frac{3}{20}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\delta\xi^{2}n\,.

Since now each vertex
v\in X^{\prime}
is associated with a unique colour
c(v)
, we may assume that one vertex part of
J^{\prime}
is just
X^{\prime}

We wish the graph
F_{t}
to be bipartite, so
let us split the set
X
as follows. Let
W_{t}\subseteq X^{\prime}
consist of vertices sampled from
X^{\prime}
independently at random with
probability
1/2
. Let
Y_{t}=X\smallsetminus W_{t}
. With positive probability,

|J^{\prime}[E({\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}^{\prime}),W_{t}]|\geq\frac{3}{50}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\delta\xi^{2}n\,,

and for each
((v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}),v)\in J^{\prime}
(recalling (
4.6
)),

(4.7)

d^{\diamond_{t}}_{c(v)}(v,Y_{t}\cap S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}))\geq 1.5\delta\xi pn\,,

where we used Chernoff bounds and the union bound. Thus we may assume that these two inequalities are satisfied.

Now, let
Z_{t}\subseteq W_{t}
be the set of vertices of degree at least
\frac{1}{50}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\delta\xi^{2}
in
J^{\prime}
, and let
J^{*}
be the induced subgraph of
J^{\prime}
on
(E({\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}^{\prime}),Z_{t})
.
Since the vertices in
W_{t}\smallsetminus Z_{t}
were incident to at most
\frac{1}{50}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\delta\xi^{2}n
edges in total, we have

|J^{*}|\geq\frac{1}{25}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\delta\xi^{2}n\,.

Recalling that
J^{*}
is a bipartite graph on the vertex sets
(E({\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}^{\prime}),Z_{t})
, we have the lower bound

(4.8)

|Z_{t}|\geq\frac{|J^{*}|}{|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|}\geq\frac{1}{25}\delta\xi^{2}n=\frac{1}{25}\delta^{1+2(t-1)}n\geq\frac{1}{25}\delta^{2t}n\,.

Now, to ensure
( )
‣
4.5
, if there is a colour
j
such that

Z_{t,j}=\{v\in Z_{t}\colon c(v)=j\}

contains at least
\alpha|Z_{t}|
vertices, let
V_{t}=Z_{t,j}
and set
\psi_{t}=j
(recalling that
\alpha\ll\delta,\ell^{-1}
is a constant).
Otherwise, set
V_{t}=Z_{t}
and
\psi_{t}=\star
.
Using the minimum degree of the vertices from
V_{t}
, we have

\big|J^{*}[E({\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}^{\prime}),V_{t}]\big|\geq\frac{1}{50}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\delta\xi^{2}|V_{t}|\,.

Moreover,
|V_{t}|\geq\alpha|Z_{t}|\geq\frac{1}{25}\alpha\delta^{2t}n
, as required by the claim.

Let
F_{t}
be the subgraph of
G
with

E(F_{t})=\{vy\colon v\in V_{t},y\in Y_{t},v\diamond_{t}y\}\,.

Recall that if
((v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1}),v_{t})\in J^{*}
, then by (
4.7
),

(4.9)

d_{F_{t}}(v_{t},S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})\cap Y_{t})\geq 1.5\delta\xi pn=1.5\delta^{t}pn\,.

Let
{\mathfrak{f}}_{t}=p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{t}}
. By Theorem
4.1
, there is a function
\mathfrak{d}_{t}
with
0\leq\mathfrak{d}_{t}\leq 1

and

\|{\mathfrak{f}}_{t}-(1+\varepsilon_{1})\mathfrak{d}_{t}\|\leq\varepsilon_{1}\,.

Let
\mathfrak{d}_{t}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{d}_{t}\!\restriction_{V_{t}\times([n]\smallsetminus(V_{1}\cup\dots V_{t}))}
, and let
D_{t}
be a graph sampled from
\mathfrak{d}_{t}^{\prime}
. By the same argument as in the induction basis, we may assume that

\left\|D_{t}-\mathfrak{d}_{t}\right\|_{\square}\leq 7\varepsilon_{1}\text{\quad and \quad}\left\|D_{t}-p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F_{t}}\right\|_{\square}\leq 9\varepsilon_{1}\,,

where
D_{t}
stands for the indicator function
\mathds{1}_{D_{t}}
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
consist of
t
-tuples
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t})
such that
v_{t}\in N_{J^{*}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})
and

(4.10)

d_{D_{t}}(v_{t},S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})\cap Y_{t})\geq\delta^{t}n\,.

Using Lemma
4.4
and (
4.9
), for each
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}^{\prime}
, there are at most
\varepsilon_{2}n
vertices
v_{t}\in N_{J^{*}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})
violating (
4.10
) (recalling that
\varepsilon_{2}>\varepsilon_{1}^{4}
), so indeed

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{H}}_{t}|

\displaystyle\geq|J^{*}[E({\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}^{\prime}),V_{t}]|-\varepsilon_{2}n^{t}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{100}|{\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}|\delta\xi^{2}|V_{t}|

\displaystyle\geq 10^{-2t}\delta^{(t-1)^{2}+2t-1}|V_{1}|\ldots|V_{t}|

\displaystyle=10^{-2t}\delta^{t^{2}}|V_{1}|\ldots|V_{t}|\,,

where we used the inductive hypothesis in the second line.

Finally, we set
S(v_{1},\ldots v_{t})=N_{D_{t}}(v_{t})\cap S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t-1})\cap Y_{t}

to obtain a set which satisfies asserting
( )
‣
4.5
of Claim
4.5
.
∎

For the remainder of the proof, we do not need properties
( )
‣
4.5
and
( )
‣
4.5
, but only the following consequences. We remark that it is crucial that the relative density of
K_{\ell}
-copies mentioned in
( )
‣
4.5
(denoted
\nu
) does not depend on
\alpha
, but only on
\delta
and
\ell
. On the other hand
|V_{i}|/n
may depend on
\alpha
.

For every subset
M\subseteq[L]
below we show

(

)

The graph
\bigcup_{i\in M}D_{i}
contains at least
\nu n\prod_{i\in M}|V_{i}|
copies of
K_{|M|+1}.

(

)

If
|M|\leq\ell
, then

p^{-\binom{\ell}{2}}\cdot\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in M}F_{i}\bigg)\geq\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in M}D_{i}\bigg)-\varepsilon_{2}\,.

We first show that part
( )
‣
4.5
of Claim
4.5
implies
( )
‣
4.3
. Fix any
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{L})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{L}
, and let
|V^{*}_{L+1}|=S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{L})
, so
|V^{*}_{L+1}|\geq\delta^{L}n
. Now, for each
v_{L+1}\in V^{*}_{L+1}
, the vertices
\{v_{i}\colon i\in M\}\cup\{v_{L+1}\}
form a clique in
\bigcup_{i\in M}D_{i}
, since
N_{D_{i}}(v_{i})
contains
v_{j}
for
j>i
by
( )
‣
4.5
.
The number of choices for
(v_{i}\colon i\in M)
contained in some edge
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{L})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{L}
is at least

|{\mathcal{H}}_{L}|\left(\prod_{i\in[L]\smallsetminus M}|V_{i}|\right)^{-1}\geq 10^{-2L}\delta^{L^{2}}\prod_{i\in M}|V_{i}|.

Putting these two bounds together, we obtain at least
n\delta^{L}\cdot 10^{-2L}\delta^{L^{2}}\prod_{i\in M}|V_{i}|
copies of
K_{|M|+1}
, which implies
( )
‣
4.3
since
L\leq 2\ell^{2}
.

Secondly, we claim that
D_{i}
satisfy
( )
‣
4.3
. Let
D=\bigcup_{i\in M}D_{i}
,
\mathfrak{d}=\sum_{i\in M}\mathfrak{d}_{i}
,
and
F=\bigcup_{i\in M}F_{i}
, so that
\mathds{1}_{F}=\sum_{i\in M}\mathds{1}_{F_{i}}
. By the triangle inequality and
part
( )
‣
4.5
of Claim
4.5
, we have

\Big\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}-(1+\varepsilon_{1})\sum_{i\in M}\mathfrak{d}_{i}\Big\|\leq\varepsilon_{1}\ell.

Hence, by Theorem
4.1
, and taking
\varepsilon_{1}
sufficiently small depending on
\varepsilon_{2}
, we have

\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F})\geq\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(\mathfrak{d})-\varepsilon_{1}\ell^{3\ell^{2}}\geq\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(\mathfrak{d})-\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{2}.

Moreover,
\left\|\mathfrak{d}_{t}-D_{t}\right\|_{\square}\leq 7\varepsilon_{1}
for
t\in[L]
, so using Lemma
4.3
, we have

\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(\mathfrak{d})\geq\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(D)-\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{2}.
It follows that

\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F})\geq\Lambda_{K_{\ell}}(D)-\varepsilon_{2}\,,

as required for the proof of
( )
‣
4.3
.

We now complete the proof of Lemma
2.2
. Let
I^{\prime}\subset[L]
be a set of order
L/2
such that
\diamond
is constant on
I^{\prime}
and, without loss of generality, we may assume that
\diamond_{i}=<
for
i\in I^{\prime}
. Moreover, let
I\subset I^{\prime}
be a set of order
\ell-1

such that

(

)

either
\psi_{i}\neq\star
for
i\in I
and
\psi
is constant or injective on
I
,

(

)

or
\psi_{i}=\star
for
i\in I
.

Let

\vartheta=\prod_{i\in I}\frac{\left|V_{i}\right|}{n},\quad F=\bigcup_{i\in I}F_{i}\text{ \quad and \quad}D=\bigcup_{i\in I}D_{i}\,,

and note that
\vartheta
is bounded from below by a constant depending on
\alpha,\delta,\ell
, due to the lower bound on
|V_{i}|

in Claim
4.5
. By assertion
( )
‣
4.3
,
D
contains at least
\nu\vartheta n^{\ell}
copies of
K_{\ell}
.
Hence, owing to
( )
‣
4.3
and
\varepsilon_{2}\leq\frac{1}{10}\nu\vartheta
, the graph
F
contains at least

\frac{1}{2}\nu\vartheta n^{\ell}p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}
copies of
K_{\ell}
.

All these copies are non-strictly min-coloured by construction of
F
(i.e.,
\varphi(uv)=c(u)
for
u<v
,
uv\in F
), and now we will use
( )
‣
4.5
and the choice of
I
to show that there is actually a strictly min-coloured or a monochromatic copy.
We first show that each copy of
K_{\ell}
in
F
has exactly one vertex in
V_{i}
for
i\in I
. Let
v_{1}<v_{2}<\dots<v_{\ell}
be the vertex set of a
K_{\ell}
in
F
, and recall the property
( )
‣
4.5
of Claim
4.5
for
F
.
Since all the edges in
F
have the starting point in
\bigcup_{i\in I}V_{i}
, we have that
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}V_{i}
. But each
V_{i}
is an independent set in
F
, so it contains at most one (and hence exactly one) vertex from
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1}\}
.

If
\psi_{i}\neq\star
for
i\in I
, any copy of
K_{\ell}
in
F
is min-coloured (in case
\psi
is injective on
I
) or monochromatic (in case
\psi
is constant on
I
); to see this, recall that by
( )
‣
4.5
of Claim
4.5
, if
uv\in E(F_{i})
,
\varphi(uv)=\psi_{i}
.

Suppose that
\psi_{i}=\star
for
i\in I
. For
i,j\in I
, let
{\mathcal{K}}_{ij}
be the collection of
K_{\ell}
-copies containing vertices
v_{i}\in V_{i}
and
v_{j}\in V_{j}
with
c(v_{i})=c(v_{j})
. We will show that for all
i\neq j\in I

(4.11)

|{\mathcal{K}}_{ij}|\leq 3\alpha\vartheta p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}.

(This follows easily from Theorem
3.1

( )
‣
3.1
when each colour class in
V_{i}
is of size
\alpha|V_{i}|
, but we need to be slightly more careful about smaller vertex classes.)

Partition the colours in
c[V_{i}]
into
clusters

1,\ldots,m
with
m\leq 2\alpha^{-1}
such that for each
k\in[m]
, the proportion of vertices in cluster
k
in
V_{i}
lies in
[\alpha,2\alpha]
. Note that such a partition exists since
|c[V_{i}]|\leq\alpha|V_{i}|
by
( )
‣
4.5
of Claim
4.5
.
For each cluster
k\in[m]
, let
\beta(k)
(resp.
\gamma(k)
) be the proportion of vertices
v
in
V_{i}
(resp.
V_{j}
) such that
c(v)
is in cluster
k
, so
\alpha\leq\beta(k)\leq 2\alpha
. By Theorem
3.1

( )
‣
3.1
, the number of
K_{\ell}
-copies with vertices in cluster
k
in both
V_{i}
and
V_{j}
is at most

(\beta(k)\gamma(k)\vartheta+\varepsilon_{1})p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}\leq(2\alpha\gamma(k)\vartheta+\varepsilon_{1})p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}.

Summing over
k\in[m]
, corresponding to clusters
1,\ldots,m
, and using
\sum_{k\in[m]}\gamma(k)\leq 1
, we obtain

|{\mathcal{K}}_{ij}|\leq\sum_{k\in[m]}(2\alpha\gamma(k)\vartheta+\varepsilon_{1})p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}\leq(2\alpha\vartheta+2\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon_{1})p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}.

Taking
\varepsilon_{1}\leq\frac{1}{3}\alpha^{2}\vartheta
implies (
4.11
).

The bound (
4.11
) holds for any
i,j
, so taking
\alpha<\nu/(4\ell^{2})
, we obtain

\left|\bigcup_{i<j\in I}{\mathcal{K}}_{ij}\right|\leq 3\ell^{2}\alpha\vartheta p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}\leq\frac{1}{4}\nu\vartheta p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}.

Recalling that
F
contains at least
\frac{1}{2}\nu\vartheta p^{\binom{\ell}{2}}n^{\ell}
copies of
K_{\ell}
, it follows that there is a copy outside
\bigcup_{i<j\in I}{\mathcal{K}}_{ij}
, which is then strictly min-coloured.

This completes the proof of Lemma
2.2
.∎

Remark 4.6
.

The fact that
K_{\ell}
is a clique was only used to show that each copy of
K_{\ell}
in
F
has at most one vertex in each
V_{i}
for
i\in I
. In the concluding remarks, we will discuss to what extent our proof extends to general graphs
H
.

5.
Concluding remarks

5.1.
Thresholds for canonical Ramsey properties for general graphs

Recall that for an ordered graph
H
, we defined
\hat{p}_{H}
as the threshold for the property
G(n,p)\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.1pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle*\hskip 1.0pt$}\vss}}}(H)
and
Theorem
1.3
establishes
\hat{p}_{K_{\ell}}=n^{-\frac{2}{\ell+1}}
. The problem of determining the threshold
\hat{p}_{H}
for ordered graphs
H
which are not complete is still open, but there are some partial results.

Firstly, Alvarado, Kohayakawa, Morris, and Mota
[
15
]
studied a closely related problem for even cycles
C_{2\ell}
. Their result implies that for
p=Cn^{-1/m_{2}(C_{2\ell})}\log n
, any colouring of
G(n,p)
contains a canonical copy of the cycle
C_{2\ell}
. However, in their work the ordering of the random graph
G(n,p)
is determined after the colouring.

Secondly, for a strictly balanced graph
H
, our proof guarantees for
p\gg n^{-2/m_{2}(H)}

a canonical copy of
H
, but one cannot require a specific vertex ordering of
H
.
This statement is shown using the following modification of Theorem
4.1
,
which actually slightly simplifies the present proof of Lemma
2.2
for
K_{\ell}
as well, but at the expense of introducing some additional formalism.
For a collection of functions
f=(f_{e})_{e\in H}
, define

\Lambda^{\dagger}((f_{e})_{e\in H})=\sum_{u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell}}\prod_{ij\in H}f_{ij}(u_{i},u_{j}),

that is, the density of
H
-copies in which the image of each edge
e\in H
is weighted by
f_{e}
.
A small modification of Corollary 3.7 in
[
4
]
(which appears in
[
3
]
in order to prove Theorem
3.1
) implies that if
\|\mathfrak{f}_{e}-\mathfrak{d}_{e}\|=o(1)
for
e\in E(H)
, then
|\Lambda^{\dagger}((\mathfrak{f}_{e})_{e\in H})-\Lambda^{\dagger}((\mathfrak{d}_{e})_{e\in H})|=o(1)
. Hence, using our proof, we can find
many
embeddings
\zeta\colon H\longrightarrow G
such that if
ij\in E(H)
with
i<j
, then
\zeta(i)\zeta(j)
lies in
F_{i}
, so its colour is determined by
\min(\zeta(i),\zeta(j))
.

5.2.
Canonical colourings in random hypergraphs

Furthermore, it would be interesting to investigate extensions of
Theorem
1.3
to
k
-uniform hypergraphs for
k\geq 3
. Namely, in their original work Erdős and Rado
[
6
]
established a canonical Ramsey theorem for
k
-uniform hypergraphs. However, their proof for
k
-uniform hypergraphs
used Ramsey’s theorem for
2k
-uniform hypergraphs and this seems to be an obstacle for transferring it
to random hypergraphs at the right threshold. Hence, for transferring their result to the random setting, it seems necessary to start with a proof which avoids the use of hypergraphs with larger uniformity. Such proofs can be found in
[
22
,
27
]
.

A.
Transference

First we informally outline the proof of Theorem 9.3 from
[
4
]
, which corresponds to our Theorem
3.1
. Then we state the formal claims that we need from
[
4
]
, and show how they are applied to deduce Theorem
4.1
. Note that the proof of Theorem 9.3 itself (as well as its corresponding deterministic result, Theorem 4.10) is not actually spelled out in
[
4
]
. Instead, the authors prove Theorem 9.1, which is a Ramsey-type result for
G(n,p)
, and say that the proof of Theorem 9.3 is ‘much the same’. Moreover, their setting is much more general – they work with random subsets of a set
X
, which for us is just the set of edges of a complete graph
K_{n}
. In particular, for us,
|X|=\binom{n}{2}
.

Theorem 9.3 from
[
4
]
states that with high probability, any subgraph
F
of
G(n,p)
with
p=Cn^{-1/m_{2}(H)}
can be approximated by a
dense model

D
which asymptotically matches the edge distribution and the number of
H
-copies in
F
.

As mentioned, instead of graphs, we work with functions from
[n]^{(2)}
to
\mathds{R}
, or weighted graphs. For a random graph
G\in G(n,p)
, the
associated measure
of
G
is defined as
\mu=\mu_{G}=p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{G}
. Given an
m
-tuple of functions

\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m})\in\mathds{R}^{[n]^{(2)}}
(which will later be taken as the associated measures of
m
independent copies of
G(n,p_{\star})
), Conlon and Gowers introduce the set of
(\mu,1)
-basic anti-uniform functions

\Phi_{\mu,1}
, which have the key property that the number of
H
-copies in a weighted graph
\mathfrak{f}\leq m^{-1}(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})
can be bounded in terms of the inner products

\max\left\{|\langle\mathfrak{f},\varphi\rangle|\colon\varphi\in\Phi_{\mu,1}\right\}\,.

The norm
\|\cdot\|
is defined as

\|\mathfrak{f}\|=\max\left\{|\langle\mathfrak{f},\varphi\rangle|\colon\varphi\in\Phi_{\mu,1}\right\}\cup\{\|\mathfrak{f}\|_{\square}\}\,,

where the term
\|\mathfrak{f}\|_{\square}
is just appended to ensure that
\|\cdot\|
also controls the edge distribution of a weighted graph. This corresponds to Definition 4.9 from
[
4
]
. Note that in order for this norm to be
useful
(that is, for the properties (P0)–(P3
′
)) below to be satisfied with high probability), it is important that
{\|\mathfrak{f}\|_{\square}}
can be expressed in terms of inner products of
\mathfrak{f}
with at most
2^{O(n)}
functions, that is,

(A.1)

\|\mathfrak{f}\|_{\square}=\max\left\{|\langle\mathfrak{f},\mathds{1}_{[U,W]}+\mathds{1}_{[U\cap W,U\cap W]}\rangle|\colon U,W\subset[n]\right\}.

One caveat in this description is that
\|\cdot\|
only controls the number of
H
-copies under certain deterministic conditions on
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}
(in the context of graphs, these conditions, denoted (P0)–(P3
′
) in
[
4
]
, imply the property that the corresponding random graphs have a sufficiently homogeneous edges distribution, which is a well-known necessary condition for all similar counting results in sparse random graphs). To prove Theorem
3.1
, Conlon and Gowers show three statements. Firstly, with high probability, the associated measures
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}
of
G(n,p_{\star})
with
p_{\star}=Cn^{-1/m_{2}(H)}
satisfy (P0)–(P3
′
)). Secondly, assuming (P0)–(P3
′
), for any
\mathfrak{f}\leq m^{-1}(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})
there is a
dense model

\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with

\|\mathfrak{f}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}\|\leq\varepsilon\,,

and thirdly,
\mathfrak{d}
is a useful approximation for
\mathfrak{f}
in our context.

These three statements imply that asymptotically almost surely, any such function
\mathfrak{f}\leq m^{-1}(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})
has a suitable dense model. To reach the same conclusion for subgraphs of
G(n,p)
, a small additional step is needed (cf. Proof of Theorem 9.1 in
[
4
]
). Given
m
independent samples of
G(n,p_{\star})
with
p_{\star}=Cn^{-1/m_{2}(H)}
, let
G
be the union of
U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{m}
. Then
G
is distributed as
G(n,p)
with
p=1-(1-p_{\star})^{m}
, which is slightly smaller than
p_{\star}m
. Thus the hypothesis
\mathfrak{f}\leq p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{G}
does not quite imply that
\mathfrak{f}\leq m^{-1}p_{\star}^{-1}(\mathds{1}_{U_{1}}+\dots+\mathds{1}_{U_{m}})=m^{-1}(\mu_{1}\dots+\dots\mu_{m})
. Still, since
p=p_{\star}m(1+o(1))
, this caveat can be resolved by slightly rescaling
\mathfrak{f}
, which we do at the start of the proof.

Now we formally state the claims which are used for deducing Theorem
4.1
, and where they can be found in
[
4
]
. Say that
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}
satisfy the property
{\mathcal{P}}(\eta,\lambda,d,m)
if they satisfy properties (P0)–(P3
′
) stated in
[
4
]
*Section 4.
The following statement can be found in the proof of Theorem 9.1 in
[
4
]
.

Lemma A.1
.

Given
\eta,\lambda,d,m
, there is
C
such that for
p_{\star}=Cn^{-1/m_{2}(H)}
the following holds. If
U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}\in G(n,p_{\star})
are mutually independent and
\mu_{i}=p_{\star}^{-1}\mathds{1}_{U_{i}}
is the associated measure of
U_{i}
for
i\in[m]
, then
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}
satisfy
{\mathcal{P}}(\eta,\lambda,d,m)
with high probability.

The following lemmata can be deduced from the proof of Theorem 4.5 in
[
4
]
.

Lemma A.2
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there are sufficiently small constants
\eta,\lambda>0
and large integers
d,m
such that if
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}
satisfy
{\mathcal{P}}(\eta,\lambda,d,m)
and
f\leq m^{-1}(\mu_{1}+\dots\mu_{m})
, then the following holds.

(

)

There is
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
with
0\leq\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\leq 1
and
\|f-(1+\varepsilon/4)\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.

(

)

If
\mathfrak{d}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
is a function with
0\leq\mathfrak{d}\leq 1
and
\|f-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}\|\leq\varepsilon
, then

\Lambda_{H}(f)\geq\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{d})-4|E(H)|\cdot\varepsilon.

Now we can deduce our desired result.

Proof outline for Theorem
4.1
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, let
\eta,\lambda,d
and
m
be as required for the conclusion of Lemma
A.2
to hold. The random graph
G
will be sampled in
m
rounds – that is, we set
p_{\star}=Cn^{-1/m_{2}(H)}
and
p=1-(1-p_{\star})^{m}\geq\left(1-\varepsilon/4\right)p_{\star}m
for sufficiently large
n
. Following the notation of Conlon and Gowers, let
U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}
be
m
mutually independent random graphs sampled independently with edge probability
p_{\star}
, where
C
is a sufficiently large constant. Our random graph
G
with edge probability
p
will then be sampled by taking the union of
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
, which indeed has the claimed distribution. For
i\in[m]
, let
\mu_{i}=p_{\star}^{-1}\mathds{1}_{U_{i}}
be the associated measure of
U_{i}
, and define
\mu=m^{-1}(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})
. Assume that
(\mu_{i})_{i\in[m]}
satisfy the property
{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}}(\eta,\lambda,d,m)
. By Lemma
A.1
, this occurs with high probability.

We will apply Lemma
A.2
to deduce the existence of
\mathfrak{d}_{F}
and part
( )
‣
4.1
. Let
F
be a subgraph of
G
, so
0\leq\mathds{1}_{F}\leq\mathds{1}_{G}\leq\sum_{i\in[m]}\mathds{1}_{U_{i}}
. Define

(A.2)

\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}=p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}\text{\quad and \quad}\mathfrak{f}=\frac{1+\varepsilon/4}{1+\varepsilon}p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}.

We claim that
\mathfrak{f}\leq\mu
. Indeed, recalling that
p^{-1}\cdot\frac{1+\varepsilon/4}{1+\varepsilon}\leq(mp_{\star})^{-1}
for large
n
, we have

\mathfrak{f}=\frac{1+\varepsilon/4}{1+\varepsilon}p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}\leq\frac{1+\varepsilon/4}{1+\varepsilon}p^{-1}\sum_{i\in[m]}\mathds{1}_{U_{i}}\leq(mp_{\star})^{-1}\sum_{i\in[m]}\mathds{1}_{U_{i}}=m^{-1}\sum_{i\in[m]}\mu_{i}=\mu\,.

Thus we can apply the above-mentioned claims.

Lemma
A.2

( )
‣
A.2
applied to
f=\mathfrak{f}
gives a function
\mathfrak{d}_{F}=\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
such that
\|\mathfrak{f}-(1+\varepsilon/4)\mathfrak{d}_{F}\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Multiplying by
\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon/4}
and recalling (
A.2
), we obtain

\|\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}_{F}\|\leq{\varepsilon}\,,

as required.

To see
( )
‣
4.1
, take
\mathfrak{d}
with
\|p^{-1}\mathds{1}_{F}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}\|\leq\varepsilon
and
0\leq\mathfrak{d}\leq 2\ell
. We have

\left\|\frac{\mathds{1}_{F}}{2\ell p}-\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}}{2\ell}\right\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2\ell}<\varepsilon\,.

We may apply Lemma
A.2

( )
‣
A.2
with
f=\frac{\mathds{1}_{F}}{2\ell p}\leq\mu
and
\mathfrak{d}
replaced by
\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{2\ell}\leq 1
to obtain

\Lambda_{H}\left(\frac{\mathds{1}_{F}}{2\ell p}\right)\geq\Lambda_{H}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{2\ell}\right)-4|E(H)|\cdot\varepsilon\,.

Using the fact that
\Lambda_{H}(\alpha\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})=\alpha^{|E(H)|}\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
for any constant
\alpha\geq 0
and any
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\colon[n]^{(2)}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
, it follows that

p^{-|H|}\Lambda_{H}(F)\geq\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{d})-4\varepsilon|H|(2\ell)^{|E(H)|}\geq\Lambda_{H}(\mathfrak{d})-4\varepsilon(2\ell)^{\ell^{2}},

as required.

Statement
( )
‣
4.1
follows from the definition of
\left\|\,\cdot\,\right\|_{\square}

and the triangle inequality. That is,

\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}\leq\left\|\mathfrak{f}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}+\left\|\varepsilon\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}\leq\left\|\mathfrak{f}-(1+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{d}\right\|_{\square}+\varepsilon\leq 2\varepsilon\,.\qed
